I argue that mandate interpretation and implementation has become highly politicised. My analysis highlights how conflicting regional, national, and local interests and expectations – particularly concerning the EACRF’s overarching strategy and the use of military force – have posed insurmountable challenges to the operation, paradoxically intensifying rather than alleviating certain local, national as well as regional tensions.\nBased on research conducted in 2022 and 2023, this article centres on how the Congolese government and Congolese non-state actors perceived the EARCF’s presence in the DRC.\nFootnote\n1\nImmersing myself in EACRF discourse, I found that regional ambivalence over peace enforcement mandates created a context-specific ‘battlefield of knowledge’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n). Given the absence of a universally accepted interpretation of norms and principles guiding regional peace efforts, normative contestation is inevitable. These normative tensions, in turn, may render such efforts susceptible to power struggles and competing interests among regional actors. Thus, while regional efforts undoubtedly constitute an increasingly important expression of African agency in peace and security (see Yaw Tchie\nCitation\n2023\n, 427), I contend their deployment in complex contexts – where actors tend to engage based on their own vested political, economic and security interests – may in fact run the risk of undermining regional peace, especially in a ‘rough neighbourhood’ (Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 43) like the Great Lakes Region. Accordingly, this article engages with the overarching themes of this special issue as outlined in the introduction (Witt\nCitation", "excerpt_sha256": "762e5f84789febc976b78315c9b3e39d3d6ec3eed57214a217bd8824756813a0", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E017", "source_id": "SRC-6a30815c9b8067e6", "url": "https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/17502977.2025.2532979", "title": "‘We Don't Want Another Observer Mission’: The East African Force and the Politics of Deployment in the DRC", "publisher": "Taylor & Francis", "raw_sha256": "2558240d4ea74e3022312568621b412fb103ce2f5e1ed6848518e5abac68f974", "fact_summary": "The EACRF's political track excluded M23 because Kinshasa rejected political dialogue with the group; the force's military deadlines passed without full M23 withdrawal, and neither the EAC military deployment nor diplomatic efforts produced measurable security improvement according to the article's cited assessment. Tshisekedi adopted a hardline stance, accused regional contingents of cohabiting with M23 and collecting taxes, and publicly escalated tensions with Rwanda during the election period.", "question_ids": ["2B.1", "2B.10", "2B.2", "2B.3", "2B.7", "8B.1", "8B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The described policy positions, accusations, and security outcomes concern 2023, with some background from late 2022.", "ruler_attribution": "The article attributes rejection of dialogue and the hardline stance to Tshisekedi's government and reports his public accusations.", "limitations": ["Some claims are reported interpretations or attributed quotations rather than independently verified findings.", "The article notes regional interests and M23/Rwanda involvement as important external factors."], "start_unit": 19, "end_unit": 25, "locator": "extracted HTML block 19 through extracted HTML block 25", "exact_excerpt": "Citation\nn.d.\n)\nThat being said, the EAC stressed that the security situation in the DRC would require a political solution. The political track would facilitate high-level negotiations with different armed groups in the context of the Nairobi and Luanda Process. Yet, these talks did not include the M23, as Kinshasa rejected any political dialogue with them (Maliro\nCitation\n2023\n). Despite this challenge, the bloc hoped to facilitate the ‘implementation of the political and military tracks’, which would be complementing each other accordingly (East African Community\nCitation\n2022b\n).\nPerhaps most importantly, the EAC clarified that if violent groups like M23 would not adhere, they would enforce compliance militarily. In a meeting between Tshisekedi and Kenyatta of November 2022 both leaders re-confirmed ‘that foreign armed groups that do not voluntarily surrender and return to their countries of origin are forcefully ejected from the territory of the DRC’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022a\n). Similarly, the Luanda-Summit at the end of November 2022 stressed that the EACRF was ready to intervene against M23 ‘in case of non-compliance by M23 to cease hostilities and withdraw from occupied territories’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022d\n). Citing an official document by the Chiefs of General Staff of the EACRF, the summit re-confirmed that ‘if M23 does not withdraw the EAC Heads of States shall authorise the use of force to compel the group to comply’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022c\n).\nThese statements demonstrate an assumed willingness to implement a military heavy interpretation of EACRF’s mandate on the ground. Yet, beyond the discursive level, this military avenue never fully transpired. Official deadlines passed, with the M23 only withdrawing partially from certain regions, recapturing other territories at the same time.\nFootnote\n10\nShortly after the summit in Luanda, the M23 announced that any ceasefire agreement between African heads of states would not concern them, as they were not included in any negotiations (Aljazeera\nCitation\n\n2022b\n). Only in late December 2022, did the M23 started pulling out of certain towns like Kibumba as a ‘goodwill gesture’ (Radio Okapi\nCitation\n2022\n). Against the backdrop of the continued presence of the M23 in Eastern DRC and the unwillingness of the EACRF to engage in combat to enforce compliance, the government in Kinshasa ultimately decided to discontinue the EAC’s mandate. Claiming that ‘the force has not achieved the expected results within the agreed deadlines,’ Patrick Muyaya, spokesperson of the Congolese government, announced the EAC’s departure for December 2023 (Actualité\nCitation\n2023\n).\nIn contrast, the EAC stressed the crucial role it played in protecting Goma and facilitating the withdrawal of the M23 along important logistical roads to Goma. The Kenyan Chief of Defence Forces General Francis Ogolla highlighted his satisfaction with the Kenyan troops, as they played their ‘role professionally with minimal collateral’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2023\n). In the following section I will analyse some of the challenges, namely the differing expectations and perceptions by regional leaders, the Congolese government and parts of Congolese civil society, around the use of military force. As my analysis will show, the actual interpretation and factual implementation of the EACRF’s mandate have remained a point of regional contestation throughout, with different regional actors competing over its normative meaning.\n‘We are not here to fight’ – The regional approach\nThe EACRF’s development and its ultimate departure from the DRC needs to be analysed against the backdrop of the bloc’s positionality in the conflict, the changing rhetoric of its prominent leaders and ultimate unwillingness to apply the use of force on the ground. From the beginning on, Rwandan President Paul Kagame (in The East African\nCitation\n2023a\n) stressed that the issue of M23 required a political approach, addressing the root causes of the underlying problems to avoid providing ‘a partial solution’. Contrary to Kenyatta’s early announcements, regional leaders substantially re-interpreted the EACRF’s supposed role in the DRC along these lines. Instead, they pushed for a strengthening of the Nairobi Process, which had started in November 2022, to demobilise armed groups (see Chome\nCitation\n2024\n).\nMoreover, some regional leaders started framing the EACRF as a non-offensive mission. In this moment, it became clear that the actual implementation of the EACRF’s mandate was, in Lie’s words, ‘open for interpretation and contestation by those acting in its name’ (\nCitation\n2012\n, 144). Stressing diplomacy, regional leaders reinterpreted the EACRF’s mandate to support the political process and monitor the ceasefire’s implementation. The following statement by Uganda’s President Museveni is illustrative in this regard:\nWe are going to be in the Bunagana-Rutshuru area, not in order to fight the M23, but to act as a neutral force as the Congolese use the time to sort out their political problems. Unless we are attacked, we are not there to fight’. (in Olukya\n\nCitation\n2023\n)\nSimilarily, the Ugandan Lieutenant General Kayanja Muhanga called attention to fundamental differences between the EACRF and the Operation Shujaa, which the UPDF had launched against ADF in November 2021. Unlike the latter, Muhanga stressed, the EACRF was not there to engage in combat but ‘to witness a process of achieving peace in Rutshuru territory and the neighbouring areas’ (The East African\nCitation\n2023c\n). As a result, the communiqué launching the third inter-Congolese dialogue under the Nairobi Process, confirmed the ‘emphasis on the Political Process [sic!] as the surest path to sustainable peace and security’ (East African Community\nCitation\n2022e\n) in November 2022.\nAlthough Kenyatta initially spoke of ‘enforcing peace,’ aligning with stabilisation and peace enforcement frameworks, Tanzania’s President Samia Suluhu Hassan later suggested that regional leaders see their commitment to peace in Eastern DRC as more closely aligned with traditional peacekeeping norms:\nEast Africa has also sent their soldiers there. They are there and their work is just to do peacekeeping. We are not going to fight, we are going to talk, to do peacekeeping, to speak with these terrorists or rebels, which threaten peace so that they agree with the regional demands.\nFootnote\n11\nThis normative re-interpretation from a robust to a rather traditional peacekeeping approach demonstrate the normative ambivalence regarding the EACRF’s mandate. It shows how regional leaders, as ‘mediators between policies and practice’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n, 145), used the operation’s normative openness to project their own interpretations of the mandate and competing visions of peace, shaped by their interests and regional positions in the DRC.\nThese normative and rhetoric shifts also translated into practice on the ground. Apart from Burundian troops, who often denied their involvement in offensive operations against the M23, the actual use of force had remained rather limited.\nFootnote\n12\nYet, this re-interpretation did neither sit well with Congolese elites nor with non-state actors. As Williams (\nCitation\n2017\n, 128) states: ‘Geographic proximity to a conflict does not automatically generate a regional consensus on how to respond’. The same applies to the DRC, where the EACRF’s mandate became highly politicised. Actors across different levels engaged in ‘knowledge battles’ (Lie\n\nCitation\n2012\n, 145) over its interpretation and implementation. While the EAC ultimately favoured political over military means and pushed for the Nairobi Process, the government in Kinshasa and many Congolese non-state actors contested this interpretation. They largely rejected any form of political dialogue and accused Kenya of siding with Rwanda (Chome\nCitation\n2024\n). As the following will illuminate, the EAC’s reluctancy to interpret the mandate militarily, led to tensions and even outright conflict between the Congolese government and the EAC’s member states.\n‘Co-Habitation was not part of the plan’ – Critique by the Congolese government\nWhen military victories failed to materialise and the M23’s compliance was not enforced militarily, it became clear that there was no shared understanding of the EACRF’s role among regional leaders, but that the mandate was in fact ‘open for interpretation’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n, 145). After protests in Goma against the EACRF in February 2023, the Congolese Deputy Prime Minister Christophe Lutundula reminded all stakeholders, ‘that the mandate of the Regional Force is unequivocally offensive’ (in Ilunga\nCitation\n2023a\n). Over the coming weeks Kinshasa continued to assert that the EACRF was not adhering to the agreed SOFA. If this continued, Lutundua warned that Kinshasa was ready to ask for the EACRF’s departure (The East African\nCitation\n2023b\n). Scaling up the rhetoric even further, Christophe Mboso, speaker of the Congolese National Assembly, stressed in March 2023 that the EACRF had been executing its offensive mandate too slowly, hereby demonstrating a lack of support for DRC. For him, the EACRF in fact offered nothing but ‘a picture of troops on holiday in the Congolese theatre of operations’ (in Ilunga\nCitation\n2023b\n).\nWhile these statements highlight the Congolese government’s normative interpretation of the EACRF’s mandate as, first and foremost offensive, the force has also faced accusations of outright collaboration with violent groups. Tshisekedi scaled up his rhetoric in May 2023:\nwe have noticed certain contingents among this force, except Burundi, who is putting all the efforts in the mission as it was defined; other contingents are now living with M23, collecting taxes in the zone that they are illegally occupying. That's a genuine problem, and it also compels us to question the purpose of the EAC mission. (in The East African\nCitation\n2023c\n)\n\nIn another statement, Tshisekedi stressed that he has noticed a certain ‘co-habitation’ between ‘the regional force of Eastern Africa and the terrorists of M23’, once again questioning the role the EAC was playing in the conflict (in Dube\nCitation\n2023\n).\nThese accusations must be understood both in the historical context of the Great Lakes Region and in light of the DRC’s internal political dynamics. As briefly stated above, certain regional leaders had historically supported different violent groups operating in the DRC, in order to safeguard their own national security and economic interests since the First and Second Congo War. As van de Walle (\nCitation\n2022\n) notes, ‘several of the DRC’s neighbours have repeatedly and deliberately undermined stability in its east by bolstering proxy fighters and tapping its huge natural resources’. Since the 1990s, there has been a constant competition over access and control over the DRC’s rich mineral resources, such as coltan and cobalt. Oftentimes this regional competition is tied to the collaboration or active support of armed groups as proxies. While the link between natural resources and conflict is complex (Vogel & Raeymaekers, 2016), neighbouring countries’ interests are significant. Many Congolese regard them as exploitation of the DRC’s wealth and a key driver of war (see IPIS, ASSODIP, and DIIS\nCitation\n2024\n; see also Birchinger, Jaw, and Witt\nCitation\n2026\nin this Special Issue).\nRwanda’s exclusion from contributing troops to the EACRF stemmed from its strained relations with the DRC, rooted in its involvement in the First and Second Congo Wars and the ongoing instability in Eastern DRC. Tensions between Rwanda and the DRC continued to rise during EACRF’s deployment, as an increasing amount of evidence suggested that Rwanda actively supports the M23 (UN Group of Experts\nCitation\n2023\n, 9–10). Accordingly, it might not come as a surprise that Tshisekedi changed his strategy from fostering stronger regional ties at the beginning of his first term towards outright confrontation (see Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 50). Particularly during run up to the December 2023 elections, he was unwilling to risk losing domestic support by engaging in dialogue with the M23, widely regarded by many Congolese as the nation's primary enemy (Congo Research Group\nCitation\n2023\n). This domestic dimension fed significantly into regional tensions. With elections approaching and President Tshisekedi in a politically weakened position, the need to demonstrate tangible results in the East became pressing. Also, it became opportune to shift blame for the persisting insecurities in the East to regional leaders and hereby deflect accountability. Accordingly, he adopted ‘a hardline stance towards the EACRF’ and intensified cooperation with local armed groups to confront the M23 (Bauma\nCitation\n\n2024\n, 9). During this time, ‘anyone advocating for negotiations was branded a traitor’ (Titeca\nCitation\n2025\n). In line with this position, President Tshisekedi escalated tensions with Kigali by publicly declaring that he would initiate a war with Rwanda if re-elected. Though this statement was made in the context of electoral campaigning, it nevertheless reflects Kinshasa’s radical stance and the escalating tensions between Rwanda and the DRC, both members of the EAC (see Musoke\nCitation\n2024\n; see also Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 52).\nAdding to these tensions with Rwanda, there is also evidence that Ugandan troops were standing by when the M23 took over Bunagana in June 2022 (UN Group of Experts\nCitation\n2022\n, 12). Stearns claims that Uganda, while fighting ADF in North Kivu, also started actively supporting the M23 in its recent fight (in Bashi\nCitation\n2022\n).\nFootnote\n13\nThough some scholars argue that Uganda’s support is much more passive than Rwanda’s, it is clear that ‘[l]eaving eastern DRC or M23 only under the influence of Rwanda is seen as a threat’ (Titeca\nCitation\n2024\n; see also Miviri\nCitation\n\n2024\n) to Kampala’s interests in the DRC.\nThese examples illustrate how regional rivalries, conflicting interests and shifting alliances have substantially shaped relations in the Great Lakes regions. Against this backdrop, it is perhaps unsurprising that the EACRF demonstrated only limited willingness – or capacity – to implement Kenyatta’s initial vision of a robust force or to fulfil Tshisekedi’s expectation of active combat engagement. While Rwanda has not been a troop contributor, Verweijen and Vogel (\nCitation\n2023\n) claim that regional leaders did shy away from a military combat because they did not want ‘to antagonize Rwanda’. Instead, they began advocating for dialogue through the Nairobi Process. As Kenya’s incumbent President Ruto stated, this approach aimed to foster a ‘greater understanding and coming together’ within the region (The East African\nCitation\n2023a\n).\nYet, this level of cooperation remained an illusion – especially given that Tshisekedi’s government has largely continued to refuse the EAC’s diplomacy-oriented approach. In this sense, one might argue that political contestation in the ‘battlefield of knowledge’ (Lie\nCitation\n2012\n) over the EACRF’s mandate turned into outright political conflict at the regional level.\nOn the ground, ‘neither the EAC military deployment nor the diplomatic efforts produced any measurable improvement in the security situation’ (Khadiagala\nCitation\n2022\n, 52). In fact, the lack of a shared normative understanding of the EACRF’s mandate and constant contestation over its role in Eastern DRC continued to deteriorate regional relations. The relationship between Kinshasa and Nairobi, in particular, reached a low point when President Tshisekedi decided to withdraw his government's ambassador from Kenya. This decision followed the formation of a new umbrella organisation called the ‘Alliance Fleuve Congo’ (AFC), which prominently included members of M23’s leadership and openly aimed to overthrow the Congolese government, capturing first Goma and then Bukavu in 2025 (Huon\nCitation\n2025\n).\nWhile the Kenyan government stated that it was proud of the work it had done in the DRC and stressed that the EACRF had in fact ‘made tremendous progress’ (East African Community Twitter Profile\nCitation\n2023\n), perceptions in Kinshasa seems to be the exact opposite. However, it was not only the relationships between regional leaders that worsened. The following section examines the criticisms of the EACRF voiced by Congolese civil society, including groups such as LUCHA, and explores how the mandate has been politicised at this level.\n‘We don’t want another observer mission’ – Perceptions of the Congolese Civil Society", "excerpt_sha256": "7f874391f1d870d7dabf81ea92ddb678bcf0c85c88decf07cd788bc8d4685c3d", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E018", "source_id": "SRC-1fe7819e54f39ddf", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/research/2024/12/drc-operation-keba", "title": "Massacre by the Army in the DRC", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "d1c4f240dbfdc5208b9ada0ebe384b4cb3322e5642314b4feb73fad2ef0d96c5", "fact_summary": "On 30 August 2023, Congolese security forces suppressed a planned Wazalendo demonstration in Goma, killing at least 56 people according to the official count, injuring more than 80, and arbitrarily arresting more than 150. Amnesty's investigation found the operation was planned in advance, that soldiers killed unarmed protesters, and that six officers were tried, with four convicted in early October.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.10", "3B.2", "3B.8", "3B.9", "4B.3", "4B.9", "7B.2", "7B.7"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "All central events occurred on 30 August and in the immediate aftermath of 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The operation was conducted by state forces under the North Kivu military command; the source does not establish a direct presidential order for the killings.", "limitations": ["The official toll conflicts with a confidential UN estimate of 102 deaths.", "Amnesty describes findings and responsibility allegations, not a final judicial determination for every implicated official."], "start_unit": 3, "end_unit": 4, "locator": "extracted HTML block 3 through extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "Français\nMassacre by the Army in the DRC\nEXECUTIVE SUMMARY\nbackground\nMassacre\nInternational response\nflawed justice\nlegal analysis\nconclusion\nPrevious\nNext\nExecutive summary\nDOWNLOAD REPORT\nAFP via Getty Images\nOn 30 August 2023, Congolese soldiers brutally suppressed a demonstration by a political and religious group in Goma, eastern Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), killing at least 56 people according to the official count, injuring over 80 and arbitrarily arresting over 150 more. The group, Natural Messianic Judaic Faith Towards the Nations – The Power of Word (FNJMN), whose followers call themselves “Wazalendo” or “Messiatha”, had been demanding the departure of the UN Mission for the Stabilization of the DRC (MONUSCO). In the wake of the massacre, authorities played down the death toll and blamed victims for the incident, describing them as “armed, drugged, and manipulated bandits playing into the hands of M23/RDF,” in reference to the Movement of 23 March (M23) armed group, which is active in North Kivu province where the city of Goma is located, and which has been backed by the Rwanda Defence Force (RDF). Faced with a public outcry after videos of the massacre emerged on social media, and following claims that the death toll was higher than they had originally acknowledged, authorities admitted that security forces had killed several dozens of people. However, they maintained that security forces intervened to restore order, claiming that the group’s followers had sowed violence in the first place, attacking the police and stoning a police officer to death. Subsequently, six officers were arrested and tried by a military court in Goma. In early October 2023, four of them, including an army commander, were convicted of murder and attempted murder.\nBetween September 2023 and October 2024, Amnesty International investigated the massacre by examining the crime sites, interviewing over 70 people, including survivors, eyewitnesses and perpetrators, and analysing court records and confidential official documents. Amnesty International’s Crisis Evidence Lab verified dozens of videos and photos of the massacre as well as satellite imagery to corroborate findings. Amnesty International was able to reconstruct the exact temporal and spatial sequence of events and identified three army officers who should be individually investigated and, if there is sufficient evidence, be prosecuted for the killings and other crimes under international law committed. These include: Lieutenant-General Constant Ndima, who, as the Governor and Military Operations Commander in North Kivu at the time of the massacre, ordered the security forces to plan the operations that led to the massacre and failed to take any action against those responsible; Colonel Mike Mikombe Kalamba, Commander of the Republican Guard’s Combined Arms Brigade (BIA) in North Kivu, whose full responsibility appears not to have been established during the Goma trial; and Major Peter Kabwe Ngandu, Special Forces Battalion Commander in Goma, who appeared to personally lead the raid at the Wazalendo radio station where several members of the group were killed, injured or detained, and who was present during the mass shooting at the “temple”\n[1]\nwhere dozens more were killed. He also oversaw Special Forces soldiers’ loading of bodies and the wounded onto trucks.\nBetween July and October 2024, Amnesty International wrote to several Congolese and UN officials, including the President of the Republic, the minister of defence, the General Military Prosecutor and the UN Under-Secretary-General for Peacekeeping Operations, sharing a summary of its key findings and requesting their response or comment. Amnesty International also wrote to the three army officers named in this report for their individual responsibility. At the time of writing, Amnesty International had received and reflected, in the report, responses from the UN Under-Secretary General for Peace Operations, the former Military Governor of North Kivu, Constant Ndima, and the former BIA Commander in North Kivu, Mike Mikombe, which are annexed to this report. Other officials and the third army officer implicated, Major Peter Kabwe, did not respond to Amnesty International’s requests.\nOperation “Keba”\nDuring previous protests between June and August 2023 in Goma, which took place without notable incident, Wazalendo’s leader, Efraimu Bisimwa, announced plans for a major protest on 30 August 2023 to demand the departure of MONUSCO and other foreign forces, accusing them of “occupying the DRC against the will of Congolese ancestors”. On 19 August 2023, he wrote to the mayor of Goma to formally give notice about the planned protest, as required by Congolese law.\nOn 21 August 2023, the Head of MONUSCO’s Goma Office wrote to the Military Governor of North Kivu, Lieutenant-General Constant Ndima, copying the mayor of Goma, expressing the UN’s concerns about the planned protests and urging him to prohibit it “given its hateful and violent nature”. Two days later, the mayor of Goma – who, since the introduction of the “State of Siege” in May 2021, has been a serving police officer – issued a statement banning the protest, saying that the FNJMN (Wazalendo) association did not have legal status to operate, even though they had held similar protests unhindered over the previous three months. On 25 August 2023, in his capacity as the Military Operations Commander in North Kivu, the Governor issued an order to army and police units under his jurisdiction to take all necessary measures to protect MONUSCO facilities and the city. Amnesty International obtained operational plans developed by the police and army in response to the governor’s instructions. One of these plans, code-named Operation “Keba” (“Watch Out”, in Lingala language), was established by the Commander of the Republican Guard’s Combined Arms Brigade, which includes the Special Forces. It was this force that would be at the heart of the massacre.\nKilling Spree\nOn 30 August, around 3am, a commando unit, which appears to have been led by Major Peter Kabwe Ngandu, Special Forces Battalion Commander in Goma, raided the FNJMN radio station in Kyeshero district, north-west of Goma. Soldiers broke into the radio station, where they arrested 12 people, including the Wazalendo leader, and confiscated transmitters and other equipment. They tied up those arrested and escorted them to the street, where army pickup trucks were parked. There, they opened fire on a group that comprised the 12 people who had been in the radio station and others who had gathered after hearing about the raid, killing six and injuring at least 10. Those killed included 44-year-old journalist Tabita Fabiola and one of the security guards who had been arrested inside the radio station building. The Wazalendo leader, Efraimu Bisimwa and five other detainees who survived the shooting were taken to the Republican Guard headquarters at Goma airport, where they were subjected to torture and other ill-treatment.\nIn the meantime, two blocks away, a police patrol had shot at Wazalendo followers who were headed to the radio station, killing one. Some of the followers captured an officer after he ran out of ammunition and took him back to their temple. At around 6am, the police shot at another group of Wazalendo followers as they left the temple, killing one of Efraimu Bisimwa’s sons, Uwezo Milele. After this incident, a group of Wazalendo followers stoned the captured police officer.\n\nCongolese soldiers, mainly from the Republican Guard’s Special Forces, deployed massively around the temple at 7am, as hundreds of Wazalendo followers gathered on a street near the temple. For about half an hour, two officers from the 34th Military Region tried to persuade the group to halt their march. At around 7.30am, the Republican Guard’s BIA Commander in North Kivu, Colonel Mike Mikombe Kalamba, cut short the talks and, according to five witnesses, including the two officers from the 34th Military Region, ordered his soldiers to open fire. Images verified by Amnesty International show bursts likely from bullets being fired towards the crowd, soldiers collecting bodies, dragging them and loading them onto army trucks. According to witnesses, officers stayed in the area for hours, cleaning puddles of blood and body parts and arresting Wazalendo followers, bystanders and residents. Before leaving the area, they looted the temple and burned it to ashes.\nUncertain Toll, Abused Bodies\nAuthorities say 56 civilians were killed. It is not clear whether this number includes those who died from injuries in the following days. Amnesty International received consistent reports of at least 10 people last seen at the Wazalendo temple on 30 August and whose whereabouts are since unknown, including one woman and her four-year-old child. A confidential UN report, seen by Amnesty International, put the death toll at 102, including 90 men, eight women and four boys, and 80 more injured. No soldier was killed or wounded. Amnesty International found no evidence that the Wazalendo followers were armed, and neither did military courts, which handed down two judgments in relation to the massacre.\nFor a fortnight, the authorities denied families access to the bodies to identify them or bury them with dignity. The bodies remained piled up in Goma’s military hospital, some in the open air. When buried three weeks later, they were in an advanced state of decomposition. No ballistics investigations or autopsies were carried out as part of investigations promised by the authorities. No DNA samples were taken for identification of 27 officially unidentified bodies. A man who identified the decomposing body of his slain nephew said: “It is as if they had killed him for a second time.”\nFlawed Justice\nBetween 1 September and 10 October, the authorities organized two separate trials before military courts in Goma: that of 116 Wazalendo followers and other related persons accused of the murder of the police officer and insurrection; and that of six Republican Guard members, including two high-ranking officers, accused of killing and wounding the Wazalendo followers. Although one officer and three soldiers were convicted in connection with these murders, Amnesty International found the investigation rushed and incomplete. The trial of the Wazalendo followers, including their collective prosecution for the murder of the police officer, also fell far short of fair trial standards.\nThis report reveals that the 30 August massacre resulted from a series of deliberate actions by the authorities, following an explicit request made to them by MONUSCO to “prohibit” the protest. The massacre was not the result of a blunder on the part of a few soldiers who intervened unexpectedly following the lynching of a police officer by the Wazalendo followers, as claimed by the authorities.\nBy reconstructing the events of 29 and 30 August 2023, the report shows that soldiers carried out the first raid targeting Wazalendo followers at around 3am at their radio station, killing at least six people and arresting others, including the Wazalendo leader. It was this raid that triggered the capture of a police officer at around 4am by a group of Wazalendo followers, and his stoning at around 6am.\nThe operation against protesters took place against the backdrop of unwarranted claims that FNJMN (Wazalendo) was not a registered organization and that their activities should, therefore, be restricted. The authorities cited this as grounds for pre-emptively banning the protest in defiance of peaceful assembly standards.\nCongolese security and defence forces should not have been deployed to police the protest as such roles should be reserved for law enforcement officials trained in policing assemblies. Firearms can only be justified as a last resort in the face of an imminent threat to life or severe injury. The soldiers deliberately killed dozens of people, both in the hours preceding the protest and during it, in violation of international human rights standards, including those governing the use of firearms.\nThese killings should be viewed in the context of widespread and/or systematic attacks against the civilian population of North Kivu and other parts of eastern DRC by both government forces and armed groups. They may, therefore, constitute crimes against humanity of murder. The situation in DRC is the subject of an investigation by the Office of the Prosecutor of the International Criminal Court (OTP),\n[2]\nwithin which the OTP recently decided to give “priority” focus to “any alleged Rome Statute crimes occurring in North Kivu since January 2022”.\n[3]\nKey Calls\nAs this report shows, the massacre in Goma on 30 August 2023 was a well-prepared operation carried out mainly by the Republican Guard, following a request from MONUSCO for a “firm intervention to prevent any threats against MONUSCO bases and staff” and to prohibit the protest “given its hateful and violent nature”. The right to peaceful protest is guaranteed by Congolese law and several international instruments ratified by the DRC. Similarly, the right to life, freedom from torture and all other rights violated in the context of the 30 August massacre in and around Goma are protected under international law. Victims of these heinous crimes have a right to truth and justice. They should not be collectively punished.\nAmnesty International is calling on President Tshisekedi to immediately suspend General Ndima and Major Peter Kabwe from the army pending investigation. Colonel Mike Mikombe, who was the Republican Guard’s Commander in North Kivu at the time and who was convicted in connection with the killings, should also be further investigated and, if there is sufficient evidence against him, prosecuted for his other actions, including torture and other ill-treatment against the Wazalendo as well as his possible liability as a superior. The DRC authorities must reopen investigations into this massacre, in line with regional and international standards, to establish the truth and hold all of those involved accountable. President Tshisekedi should further take urgent steps to specify the exceptional circumstances under which the Republican Guard can participate in law-enforcement activities, in line with international standards. Lastly, the president must lift the “State of siege” in North Kivu and Ituri provinces and, together with parliament, take urgent steps to limit the involvement of the army in law enforcement missions. As for the Office of the Prosecutor of the International Criminal Court, it should consider examining the 30 August 2023 killings in Goma as part of the ongoing investigation into the situation in eastern DRC.\nMONUSCO’s request to the Congolese authorities to prohibit the Wazalendo protest failed to give appropriate emphasis either to the presumption that assemblies should be considered peaceful (in the absence of compelling evidence to the contrary) or to the State’s obligations to facilitate and protect the assembly. This request provided the immediate context for the violation by the Congolese authorities of the right to peaceful assembly as protected under international human rights law. MONUSCO’s statement in the immediate aftermath of the massacre was also deficient as it appeared to justify the response of the authorities to the demonstration and to place blame on the protest’s organizers. It stated that the protest had not been authorized – which is not required under the DRC law – adding that “in accordance with their primary responsibility for security in the country, the Congolese defence and security forces” “attempted to prevent the demonstration that was going to be violent, according to its organisers”. Amnesty International calls on the UN Department for Peace Operations to promptly investigate the role played by MONUSCO and its leadership in the events that led to the massacre.\nAmnesty International\nAn empty lot with rubble where the Wazalendo temple used to stand.\nMethodology\nThis report is based on an in-depth investigation by Amnesty International between September 2023 and October 2024, which included over 70 interviews, reconstructing the sequence of events with dozens of photos, videos and documents analysed, including confidential government and UN files, and court records.\nFrom 30 August, Amnesty International’s Crisis Evidence Lab verified at least 80 photos and videos of the massacre and surrounding events. Combined with satellite imagery, field visits and eyewitness testimonies, these images made it possible to reconstruct the temporal and spatial sequence of events.\nAmnesty International travelled to Goma between 22 September and 1 October 2023, conducting on-site research and 59 interviews, including 42 individually and others in focus groups. A further 12 interviews were conducted by a Goma-based research partner and by voice call between 30 August and 30 November 2023. Survivors, eyewitnesses, victims’ relatives, medical professionals, defence and victims’ lawyers, military prosecutors, UN officials, mortuary and cemetery personnel, journalists, military experts, and other persons of interest were interviewed, mainly in Kiswahili and French. 11 interviewees were women.\nAmnesty International analysed around 100 pages of court records, official documents, as well as confidential reports from the Congolese authorities and MONUSCO. Researchers also analysed audio and video recordings of hearings in the first instance trials of the Wazalendo before the Goma Garrison Court, as well as that of four rank-and-file soldiers and two officers of the Republican Guard before the North Kivu Military Court. The appeal trials, which were pending at the time of publication, lie outside the scope of this report.", "excerpt_sha256": "8aedd2a3b285f31a8763e028e1d8a83010b9be1d7ea316881eb143802f820b7c", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E019", "source_id": "SRC-1fe7819e54f39ddf", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/research/2024/12/drc-operation-keba", "title": "Massacre by the Army in the DRC", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "d1c4f240dbfdc5208b9ada0ebe384b4cb3322e5642314b4feb73fad2ef0d96c5", "fact_summary": "Tshisekedi's government used a militarized security approach in eastern DRC: it maintained the state of siege, authorized regional military interventions, mobilized local armed groups and private military companies, criticized MONUSCO, and in August 2023 used army operational orders to ban and suppress the Wazalendo protest. The orders characterized protesters as enemy auxiliaries and authorized the Republican Guard to act in the operation.", "question_ids": ["2B.2", "2B.4", "2B.5", "3B.5", "3B.8", "3B.9", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The state-of-siege policy and military approach predated 2023 but were implemented and intensified during 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The source directly attributes the broader approach to Tshisekedi's government and identifies the Republican Guard as reporting directly to the president.", "limitations": ["The source describes legal and operational authority but does not prove that Tshisekedi personally approved the specific Goma orders.", "The security context included ongoing M23 attacks and regional intervention constraints."], "start_unit": 5, "end_unit": 9, "locator": "extracted HTML block 5 through extracted HTML block 9", "exact_excerpt": "In July 2024, Amnesty International wrote to several Congolese and UN officials, including the President of the Republic, the minister of defence, the minister of interior, the minister of justice, the General Military Prosecutor as well as the UN Under-Secretary-General for Peace Operations, sharing a summary of its key findings and requesting their response or comment. Amnesty International also wrote to the three army officers named in this report for their individual responsibility. At the time of publication, Amnesty International had received responses from the UN Under-Secretary-General for Peace Operations, from the former Military Governor of North Kivu, Constant Ndima, and from the former Commander of the Republican Guard in North Kivu, Mike Mikombe. Amnesty International reviewed their responses, which are annexed to this report, and updated its findings accordingly.\nback to top\nBackground to the Carnage\nAFP via Getty Images\nArmed Conflict, Foreign Intervention and Popular Uprising\nThe UN Stabilization Mission in the DRC, MONUSCO, deployed since 1999,\n[4]\nhas often been unable to protect civilians from attacks by armed groups, including M23, and sometimes by the Congolese army itself.\n[5]\nWhen he came to power in 2019, DRC’s President Félix Tshisekedi pledged to improve security and civilian protection in eastern DRC.\n[6]\nOpting for an essentially militaristic approach, he gave a more prominent role to the Congolese army and sought military interventions from countries in the region, while criticizing MONUSCO and pushing for its withdrawal.\n[7]\nHe declared a “State of siege” (similar to a state of emergency) in Ituri and North Kivu provinces in May 2021, granting extraordinary powers to the army and the police, claiming it was a means to rapidly restore security.\n[8]\nThe measure was continually extended and used by the army and police to undermine human rights.\n[9]\nLater that year, the Tshisekedi administration authorized the intervention of the Ugandan army in parts of North Kivu and Ituri,\n[10]\nand, in June 2022, that of the East African Community Regional Force (EACRF).\n[11]\nIn May 2023, after criticizing the failure of the EACRF,\n[12]\nhe requested the intervention of the Southern African Development Community (SADC), which was deployed to North Kivu in December 2023.\n[13]\n\nThe DRC government has openly mobilized local armed groups\n[14]\nand resorted to private military companies\n[15]\nto support the army since 2023, amid persistent war rhetoric between the DRC and Rwanda. Congolese authorities accuse Rwanda of aggression and sponsoring M23 rebels.\n[16]\nThe M23 rebels have occupied parts of North Kivu province since November 2021, despite regional and international efforts for disarmament.\n[17]\nFaced with persistent insecurity despite interventions by the UN and regional forces, the population has regularly protested against the presence of these forces. During anti-MONUSCO protests in Goma and other towns in July 2022, security forces killed at least 32 people, while four UN peacekeepers were killed by protesters, according to the government.\n[18]\nThis is the context in which FNJMN emerged in early 2023. Although the political and religious group’s statements and actions began to be widely publicized around June 2023, it had been legally registered as a non-profit association and operating in Goma since 2019, according to official registration documents seen by Amnesty International and testimonies from members of the group.\n[19]\nIt advocates a return to traditional Congolese and African spiritual practices and “rejection of imperialism in all its guises”.\n[20]\nFollowers of this group are called Wazalendo, which means “patriots” in Swahili.\n[21]\nBefore its protest planned for 30 August 2023, the group held three peaceful protests in Goma on 30 June, 30 July and 2 August 2023.\n[22]\nThese protests, at least two of which were at MONUSCO’s headquarters, took place without violence\n.\n[23]\nPlans for Protest and Authorities’ Reaction\nDuring previous protests in June and July, Efraimu Bisimwa, Wazalendo’s leader, announced a “major protest for 30 August” to demand MONUSCO’s departure. For weeks, he repeated this call via local radio stations, social media, and during the group’s worship services, mobilizing his followers and the public to participate.\n[24]\nOn 19 August, he wrote to the Mayor of Goma to formally give notice about the planned protest as required by Congolese law.\n\n[25]\nHe said: “As usual, it will be a silent and peaceful march. We will depart from our temple and head to the MONUSCO headquarters. Our ancestors will protect us from anyone who might be tempted to attack us.”\n[26]\nMONUSCO’s Request to Prohibit Protest\nOn 21 August, the Head of MONUSCO’s Goma Office wrote to the North Kivu Governor Lieutenant-General Constant Ndima, expressing concerns about the planned protest and urging him to prevent it from taking place.\n[27]\nThe letter stated: “the [Wazalendo’s] call is “a clear incitement to violence against MONUSCO and also against the authorities if they were to interfere with this mobilization. […]. We remain concerned about the motivations of these ‘Wazalendo’ and the FNJMN in particular, as well as the security risks that their actions could pose not only to our bases and staff but also to the Congolese security services and the established authorities.”\n[28]\nIn what appears to be an implicit reference to the conflict with M23 and DRC’s tensions with Rwanda, she went on to reference “a manipulation” which could “create instability and vulnerability that will be difficult to contain at a time when your own efforts are aimed at containing insecurity and anticipating threats in the province”.\n[29]\nThe letter requested the governor’s “firm intervention” with the Wazalendo “to prevent any threats against MONUSCO bases and staff”, and urged him to prohibit the protest “given its hateful and violent nature”.\n[30]\nA letter sent by the head of MONUSCO’s Goma office to the governor of North Kivu on 21 August 2023. Highlights and translations added by Amnesty International.\nOn 28 August, the governor wrote back to MONUSCO’s Head of Goma Office, reassuring her that he had instructed the security forces “to make the necessary arrangements” regarding her request.\n[31]\nIn response to Amnesty International’s findings and questions, the UN Under-Secretary-General for Peace Operations, said that MONUSCO’s request to the authorities to prohibit the protest followed exchanges of information with the authorities which indicated that “the actions planned for 30 August posed significant risks to both peacekeepers and residents in Goma”.\n[32]\nHe stressed that “the Mission’s communications emphasized the need for the Congolese authorities to proactively address these concerns to prevent any loss of life and were delivered within the framework of regular dialogue, stressing the importance of respecting human rights while avoiding undue restrictions on the freedoms of expression and peaceful assembly.”\n[33]\nHe stated: “Notable examples include: a letter dated 30 June from Mr. Bisimwa, leader of the Faith Natural Judaic Messianic towards the Nations (FNJMN), addressed to President Félix Tshisekedi, inciting violence against MONUSCO and other United Nations agencies; a public communication from FNJMN on 18 July to the Mayor of Goma reiterating their intention to erect a statue of Patrice Lumumba on the land occupied by the Mission’s logistics base in Goma; protests organized by FNJMN on 30 and 31 July in front of the Mission’s logistics base; the broadcast of hostile messages targeting MONUSCO disseminated on 2 August on FNJMN’s community radio, Uwezo wa Neno; as well as messages affiliated with Wazalendo and FNJMN circulated on social media, calling for a widespread campaign against MONUSCO starting on 30 August.”\n[34]\nAmnesty International reviewed most of FNJMN’s statements and messages in the lead-up to 30 August 2023, including Bisimwa’s 30 June letter to President Tshisekedi, his 18 July letter to the Mayor of Goma, as well as several social media posts, but could not find evidence of incitement to violence. On 11 October 2024, Amnesty International sent a follow up letter to the UN Under-Secretary-General for Peace Operations, requesting additional information to support his claims.\n[35]\nThe letter reiterated unanswered questions regarding the assessments undertaken by MONUSCO, if any, to determine whether and how the risks identified might have been mitigated without resorting to an outright prohibition of the planned protest, and in relation to the possible consequences of requesting the Congolese authorities to prohibit the protest. Amnesty International further reiterated its question about the reasons why the UN did not publish the findings of its own investigations into these events. At the time of publication, the UN had not responded to our follow up requests.\nPreemptive Ban\n\nOn 23 August, the Mayor of Goma released a statement announcing that the 30 August protest planned by the Wazalendo was banned because of the group’s registration status.\n[36]\nSeveral letters followed between him and the Wazalendo leader, with copies sent to other authorities, including the governor. In his letters, the mayor claimed that the protest was banned because Wazalendo was not legally registered as a non-profit association and because of the “State of siege”.\n[37]\nHowever, Efraimu Bisimwa provided evidence that FNJMN had been legally registered as a non-profit association since 2019 and insisted that the protest go on as planned.\n[38]\nIn any event, authorities were not justified in banning the protest on the basis of Wazalendo’s registration status.\n[39]\nIn an interview with Amnesty International, Efraimu Bisimwa stated that they reached a compromise after a police official went to see them and urged them to call off the protest.\n[40]\nHe said:\n“On the evening of 28 August, Colonel Thierry arrived at our temple at around 4pm. He had reached us thanks to our follower, Mama Deborah, who is close to him. We had a three-hour meeting with him as he tried to convince us not to hold the protest on 30 August. I told him that the followers were largely mobilized and that it was downright difficult for nothing to be done on that day. In the end, I suggested that, as a police commander, he sends a few police officers to escort a small delegation of ours that would go and deliver our message to the authorities on 30 August. That was our final compromise. Colonel Thierry even sat down with us around the fire as we left the temple. He took part in some of our rituals, saying that he was also the son of a traditional chief. He even drank the Kasiksi [a local beer made from bananas] with us, which is a sign of the covenant. For us, this meant that the agreement had been reached and sealed by all parties.”\n[41]\nOperational Plan\nMeanwhile, at the request of the governor, the army was in the process of planning and mounting an operation against the Wazalendo. In an official message (“télégramme”) sent to the army and police units under his jurisdiction on 25 August, in his capacity as Military Operations Commander, Governor Lieutenant-General Ndima characterized the Wazalendo as “a pressure group which supports M23 and the Rwanda Defence Force terrorists”, and whose aim was to “sow unrest in the town in order to facilitate enemy infiltration.”\n[42]\nHe ordered them to take all necessary steps to protect MONUSCO facilities and Goma.\n[43]\nAmong those copied in the message were the defence minister, the army chief of staff and the head of the President’s military household.\nA message signed by the governor of Nord-Kivu addressed to the 34th Military Region. Highlights and translations added by Amnesty International.\nFollowing the governor’s message, the 34th Military Region issued an operational order on 28 August to all army units in Goma, including the Republican Guard’s Combined Armed Brigade (BIA).\n[44]\nThe order stated that “according to information in our possession, the so-called Wazalendo pressure group, which is increasingly acting as an auxiliary of the M23-RDF movement, is planning a march on 30 August 2023 in the town of Goma to demand the departure of MONUSCO, while sowing unrest in order to facilitate enemy infiltration into the town” (emphasis added).\n[45]\nThe operational order characterized the Wazalendo as a pressure group engaging in a march. The instructions were to “secure and permanently defend the city of Goma by reinforcing the CltZ [military area control] apparatus, protect MONUSCO facilities and disperse protesters”.\n\n[46]\nWhile the order recalled that rules of engagement and behaviour are strict, including the ban on the use of firearms “except in self-defence”,\n[47]\nits instructions did not recognize or refer to the right of peaceful assembly or reflect the obligation of law enforcement authorities to protect and facilitate the exercise of this right.\nA document containing orders of operation for the 34th Military Region. Highlights and translations added by Amnesty International.\nIn the order’s annex, each unit involved, including the Republican Guard/BIA, is given precise instructions in three phases corresponding to the response on 28, 29 and 30 August.\n[48]\nIn the final phase (30 August), their mission was to: “pursue the enemy”, “destroy isolated enemy elements”, “consolidate the area”, and “reorganize”.\n[49]\nIn response to the operational order, on 29 August, the Commander of the Republican Guard’s Combined Arms Brigade, Colonel Mike Kalamba Mikombe, issued a detailed “Motorised Deterrence Patrol Order” code-named “Keba”,\n[50]\nto units under his direct command, including the elite Special Forces (FS)\n[51]\nThe document set out the same allegation of collusion between the Wazalendo and the M23 and the Rwanda Defence Force and specified that it had been drawn up on the “orders/authorisation of the higher level.”\n[52]\nBIA’s objective, as stated in the document, was to “carry out a motorised deterrence patrol in the main roads around the city of Goma” on 30 August between 4am and 11.30am and to “nip in the bud the attempt [to sow chaos] by the Messianic pressure group.” While the order recalled the ban on the use of firearms, it included broader exceptions: “except upon order or in case of self-defence.”\n[53]\nA document containing orders of operation for the Republican Guard. Highlights and translations added by Amnesty International.\nOn 28 August, the police also drew up a detailed “service order”\n[54]\nfor their own units, but according to the Mayor of Goma (who, since the “State of siege”, is also a police officer), the service order was not implemented “because the army had taken the lead”.\n[55]\nback to top\n30 AUGUST: The Massacre\nAFP via Getty Images", "excerpt_sha256": "0f753a3aa966bf73bf92ca1c33a04cd727dbaec6bfb11fc1201c8bb3a6b3ed7f", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E020", "source_id": "SRC-1fe7819e54f39ddf", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/research/2024/12/drc-operation-keba", "title": "Massacre by the Army in the DRC", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "d1c4f240dbfdc5208b9ada0ebe384b4cb3322e5642314b4feb73fad2ef0d96c5", "fact_summary": "Authorities initially reported six Wazalendo deaths and 158 arrests, later reported 43 deaths, and ultimately recorded 56 deaths; a confidential UN report cited by Amnesty estimated 102 dead and 80 wounded. At least ten people remained missing, bodies were held for weeks without proper identification procedures, no ballistic investigations or autopsies were conducted, and families were denied timely access and dignified burial arrangements.", "question_ids": ["3B.1", "3B.4", "3B.9", "7B.1", "7B.2", "7B.6", "7B.7"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The casualty reporting, missing-persons problems, investigative failures, and burial practices occurred in 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The failures are attributed to Congolese authorities and military justice institutions, not directly to Tshisekedi personally.", "limitations": ["The death toll remains disputed.", "Amnesty's findings concern investigative adequacy and human-rights obligations, not a final criminal judgment."], "start_unit": 17, "end_unit": 19, "locator": "extracted HTML block 17 through extracted HTML block 19", "exact_excerpt": "But we could not find him among those detained, nor among the wounded in hospitals, nor even among the dead.\n“Riziki” (name changed for security reasons), 36, sister-in-law of a person missing since the massacre.\nThe official death toll evolved over time. On the morning of 30 August, the governor’s office issued a press release stating that one police officer had been stoned to death and six Wazalendo followers had been killed, “some wounded, and 158 more arrested.”\n[123]\nOn 31 August, an internal army report cited by Agence France Presse gave a much higher toll: 48 protesters “neutralized”(meaning “killed” in security jargon), a police officer “lynched”, 75 protesters injured, and “around 168 people arrested.”\n[124]\nA few hours after this report was leaked to the press, the Congolese government issued a press release stating that 43 people had died, 56 persons were injured, and 158 were arrested. According to military court records, as of 18 September, when the official burial took place, 56 people had been counted dead in connection with the 30 August events, not including the police officer,\n[125]\nand their bodies had been held in three hospitals as follows:\n43 bodies at the Goma military hospital, including 29 formally identified and 14 unidentified;\nFour bodies at Heal Africa Hospital;\nNine bodies at CBCA Ndosho Hospital.\nAt the official burial site, however, Amnesty International only observed 54 graves.\n[126]\nAsked about this discrepancy in an interview with Amnesty International, the military prosecutor said that he had information that some bodies had been directly handed to families by hospitals, and that he was investigating this.\n[127]\nA confidential UN Joint Human Rights Office (UNJRO) report, seen by Amnesty International, put the death toll at 102 dead, including 90 men, eight women and four boys, as of 11 October.\n[128]\nWhat the wounds tell about the mass shootings\nAmnesty International reviewed medical reports from three Goma hospitals about these events, covering 84 cases of gunshot wounds, including 28 with brief descriptions of the wounds.\n[129]\nThey suggest that soldiers aimed for vital parts of victims’ bodies, hitting them in the head, arms, abdomen, thorax and back. Heal Africa, a local hospital specializing in the treatment of serious injuries, admitted seven of the most seriously wounded who were transferred from the Goma Military Hospital.\n[130]\nIts report mentions the case of Mathene Busala, a pregnant woman of an undetermined age who was admitted with multiple firearm wounds, including a haemothorax (accumulation of blood within the pleural cavity), open injury to the abdomen, and fracture of the pelvis. “She just passed away during the writing of this report,” the report says.\n[131]\n\nAnother case was that of Muhindo Buroko, a 53-year-old man admitted to intensive care at Heal Africa Hospital with “open trauma to the chest by firearm with massive haemothorax.”\n[132]\nBy the evening of 1 September, hospitals had confirmed nine deaths due to injuries and had three victims in intensive care.\n[133]\nAmnesty International also spoke with relatives of five out of ten people who were reportedly unaccounted for since 30 August, in connection with these events.\n[134]\nAmong them is Antoinette (family name withheld for security reasons) aged 38, along with her four-year-old daughter Francine. Antoinette’s sister-in-law told Amnesty International: “On the morning of 29 August, [Antoinette] arrived in Goma with her baby by boat from the village of Buhozi, to attend the Wazalendo celebration that was scheduled for 30 August 2023. I personally went to see her at the port because she had told me by phone that she had a parcel of food from the village for me. After handing me the parcel, she left for the temple, promising to come and visit my family after their feast day. She never came back and never returned to the village. We went around morgues, detention centres and hospitals, but never found her.”\n[135]\nWamba is Antoinette’s older brother. He said: “She was the mother of four, and her husband died not long ago. Until now I don’t know what to tell my little nephews. The family doesn’t know how to mourn. The authorities won’t tell us anything.”\n[136]\nAnother relative of a missing person said:\n“My 20-year-old brother-in-law Rodrigue Busimwa lived with me. He was a fervent member of the Wazalendo and even sang in one of their choirs. He had been spending the night at their temple for four days to prepare for their big celebration on 30 August. But since then, he has never returned home. We could not find him among those detained, or among the wounded in hospitals, or even among the dead.”\n[137]\n28-year-old Shabantu Kasisi was one of the Wazalendo followers delegated to talk with army officers, and who, according to several witnesses, was among the first to be shot dead in the mass shooting. His sister and two eyewitnesses unequivocally identified him in one video linked to the shooting at the Wazalendo temple.\n[138]\nPointing to a man wearing a black t-shirt and peacefully engaging with soldiers, the sister told Amnesty International: “It is him. You can see how he was calming other protesters. Why did they kill him? What happened to his body? I have so many questions, but will anybody ever answer them? Up to now, our mother has refused to accept that Shabantu is dead until she sees his body with her own eyes. I am afraid she could die of anguish.”\n[139]\nRelatives identified Shabantu Kasisi talking to security officers in videos filmed on the day of the massacre. He has been missing since then.\nThe wife of another missing person said that she saw her husband’s body being dragged by soldiers in the street after the shooting in one of the videos. She explained that they visited all morgues in Goma trying to find his body, to no avail.\n[140]\nAccording to one of their lawyers, authorities did little to nothing to address claims about missing persons and bodies during the soldiers’ trial.\n[141]\nBodies Left to Rot\nAccording to the North Kivu Military Court, 43 victims’ bodies were taken to the military hospital morgue on 30 August. Eyewitnesses told Amnesty International that at least 42 bodies were stacked and wrapped in tarpaulins, where they remained for at least two weeks, regardless of whether it was sunny or raining.\n[142]\n\nOne morgue employee said:\n“The hospital’s morgue has a capacity of only 15 people, and it was already full. All we could do was to embalm them regularly with formaldehyde and hope that they would be buried quickly.”\n[143]\nAt first, military authorities prevented relatives of those killed from seeing the bodies and trying to identify their loved ones, and they were not permitted to take them for mourning and family burial. One man who managed to see the bodies on 30 August thanks to a family acquaintance in the army described the situation:\n“The bodies were piled one on top of the other on a tarpaulin, like garbage bags. Two morgue employees helped remove the bodies from above and placed them on the side. I recognized my nephew by the red t-shirt and the blue jeans he was wearing. A bullet had pierced his back and exploded his chest. His face was wrinkled but intact. I thanked them and left to inform the family so that we could start the mourning”.\n[144]\nNearly two weeks after the massacre, authorities finally allowed families to identify their loved ones.\n[145]\nBy then, it was too late, as they were in putrefaction because they had not been properly preserved. Four people who saw the bodies told Amnesty International that the smell was unbearable and identification almost impossible.\nOne lawyer who had accompanied one of his clients for identification said: “They had bloated; the skin had turned white, just getting close to it was unbearable.”\n[146]\nThe man who had been fortunate enough to see his nephew’s body on 30 August had to return and try to identify his nephew because his body had not been tagged. He said:\n“This time, the bodies were wrapped in individual mortuary bags and placed in rows on the tarpaulins. Morgue employees took people around, opening one bag after another until you identified your relative. The smell was unbearable, but I plucked up the courage and took it in stride. Jean-Claude was no longer the same. I only recognized him by the trim of his beard and his belt. The rest was totally unrecognizable. I broke down in tears.”\n[147]\nMany relatives of those killed were denied the right to identify and bury their loved ones with dignity and according to their faith. Bodies of those killed were dragged on the ground, unceremoniously piled onto a truck, and then left to rot in a hospital yard for three weeks, much to the chagrin of their families.\nUndignified Burials\nSeveral attempts by the Wazalendo and civil society groups in Goma to hold public mourning ceremonies were banned or violently dispersed. For example, on 1 September, the police dispersed a public mourning ceremony held near Stade de l’Unité by beating mourners with sticks and firing tear gas, injuring at least two participants, and arbitrarily arresting activist Josué Kabanza.\n[148]\nHe was released without charge on 4 September, following protests by other activists.\n[149]\nThe government decided to proceed with a burial on 18 September for the 56 people whose deaths had been officially recorded.\n[150]\nSix victims’ relatives told Amnesty International that they were not consulted or informed in advance. One man whose brother was among those killed and identified said: “On Sunday evening, I listened to a communiqué from the Mayor of Goma on the radio, asking families to go to the military hospital the next day in the morning for the burial of the Wazalendo. They did not even dare to call or send us an SMS. There are family members who had wished to attend but could not because they did not have the information in advance.”\n[151]\nThose detained were not given a chance to mourn their loved ones, despite their request through their lawyers.", "excerpt_sha256": "b57833c206f3e093a964d7f2cc4cd4d87745274372006512c6a889ce4c23cdae", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-E021", "source_id": "SRC-1fe7819e54f39ddf", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/research/2024/12/drc-operation-keba", "title": "Massacre by the Army in the DRC", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "d1c4f240dbfdc5208b9ada0ebe384b4cb3322e5642314b4feb73fad2ef0d96c5", "fact_summary": "Military authorities prosecuted 116 Wazalendo followers and convicted 64, including four sentenced to death, while the separate trial of security personnel convicted one commander and three soldiers. Amnesty found the prosecutions of civilians lacked individualized evidence, the investigations were incomplete, victims and defense witnesses were restricted, and the court recognized only 109 victims and ordered USD 100,000 in reparations.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "3B.5", "3B.8", "3B.9", "4B.4", "7B.2", "7B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The arrests, trials, convictions, and reparations order occurred between September and December 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The proceedings were conducted by state military authorities; the source does not establish Tshisekedi's direct role in the judicial decisions.", "limitations": ["Amnesty characterizes the proceedings as flawed while acknowledging convictions of some security personnel.", "Appeals were pending at the time of the report."], "start_unit": 22, "end_unit": 25, "locator": "extracted HTML block 22 through extracted HTML block 25", "exact_excerpt": "AFP via Getty Images\nJustice… What justice! All this was nothing but a PR stunt to dissipate pressure and to be able to tell anyone who brings up the topic: the officer responsible was punished and justice was served.\nA lawyer for the victims\nSurvivors in the Dock\nOn 30 August, as authorities congratulated themselves on “foiling a plot against Goma”, they announced the arrest of 158 people whom they described as “armed bandits, drugged, in the pay of M23 and Rwandan terrorists.”\n[172]\nTwo days later, 13 of them were released, while 145 others, including 29 children, were brought before the Garrison Military Court of Goma and charged with “participation in an insurrectional movement”, “criminal conspiracy”, and “murder.”\n[173]\nThe 29 children were transferred to the juvenile justice system, which dropped charges and released them in mid-September. Meanwhile, 116 adults, including 102 men and 14 women, remained in detention as they were brought to trial.\n[174]\nOn 9 October, the Garrison Military Court of Goma found 64 of them guilty of “participation in insurrectional movement”, “murder” and “attempted murder” of police officers. The court sentenced Efraimu Bisimwa and three other Wazalendo leaders to death, while 60 followers were sentenced to 10 to 20 years in prison.\n[175]\nThe court acquitted 52 people who were subsequently freed from prison.\n[176]\nThe court prosecuted the 116 Wazalendo followers, 64 of whom it convicted of “participation in insurrectional movement” solely because they belonged to the religious and political group or because they planned or participated in the 30 August protest, which was meant to be peaceful. The court ruled that spending the night at the Wazalendo temple when the 30 August protest had been banned by the mayor of Goma was proof of participation in an insurrectional movement.\n[177]\nYet, in the separate trial of the officers and soldiers for killing the protesters, the North Kivu Military Court dismissed the officers’ claims that the Wazalendo were armed or used the gun they took from a police officer, thereby rejecting that there was any armed resistance or actions that could constitute insurrection.\n[178]\nRegarding the charges of “criminal participation” in the murder of a police officer, the Garrison Court of Goma’s judgment did not show how each of the 64 convicts participated in the stoning of the police officer nor provided any evidence whatsoever in this regard.\n[179]\nNot all these people, if any, could have individually participated in the stoning of the police officer. Therefore, for at least some of these people, their sole crime was to be or to be perceived to be members of the Wazalendo, to have participated in the protest and/or to have found themselves in the path of soldiers during the crackdown. A resident of Nyabushongo who witnessed intimidation and arbitrary arrests by Special Forces soldiers in his neighbourhood after the massacre told Amnesty International: “After the mass shooting, the Hiboux [Special Forces soldiers] went into the shops and houses in the neighbourhood, confiscating smartphones and arresting people, especially men. It was as if, for them, everyone in the neighbourhood was Wazalendo, and that was a crime.\n[180]\nI saw them arrest four young men in a shop. They released three of them, but they took the fourth away, and he is still in prison.”\n[181]\nThis was corroborated by the Garrison Military Court of Goma, which found that at least 52 out of the 116 people it prosecuted had been arrested by soldiers outside of any protest. This included cases where individuals were apprehended in their homes and shops.\n\n[182]\nDespite this, the court alleged that while many may not have directly participated in the stoning of the police officer, they must have encouraged and approved of it by their presence at the crime scene and were, therefore, guilty.\n[183]\nAs for the “attempted murder” conviction, the court relied solely on the testimonies of three police officers who claimed that they had been attacked and wounded by a group of Wazalendo followers while on foot patrol, in the incident in which their colleague was kidnapped, taken to the Wazalendo temple, and later stoned to death.\n[184]\nThey identified two Wazalendo followers whom they said were part of the group which attacked them.\n[185]\nNo further evidence was adduced to support these claims and, above all, to determine the individual responsibility of each of the accused.\nAmnesty International is of the view that the Garrison Military Court of Goma failed to sufficiently demonstrate that the 64 convicts had individually participated in the murder of police officer Kipapa Safari or were even present when he was stoned. In addition, civilians should never be tried by military courts.\n[186]\nThey should either be immediately released or retried in conformity with fair trial standards before competent civilian courts and without the recourse to the death penalty.\nThese proceedings against the survivors of the 30 August massacre largely appear to be an attempt by the military justice system to primarily hold the Wazalendo leader and followers responsible for the violence that took place on 30 August in Goma and its consequences, rather than an attempt to genuinely investigate and prosecute those responsible for the stoning of the police officer.\nOne of their lawyers summed up what their prosecution meant to them:\n“These are survivors of carnage. About half of them were bystanders, neighbours, and other people unrelated to the Wazalendo. Many were tortured during or after their arrest. One woman was completely undressed. Those injured were not treated. Many lost a family member in the massacre. Bisimwa himself lost two of his children. Yet they were the ones who were brought to justice, called terrorists, and humiliated. Worse, they are being judged by the army that attacked them, and with the assistance of MONUSCO, against whom they were about to protest. Do you think this qualifies as justice?”\n[187]\nThose convicted appealed, and their case before the North Kivu Military Court started on 29 December 2023. At the time of writing, the appeal trial was still ongoing.\nPerpetrators’ Accountability\nAs the Wazalendo were on trial, many people denounced the authorities’ eagerness to arrest and prosecute survivors of the massacre rather than holding the military accountable.\n[188]\nIt was not until five days after the massacre and because of mounting public pressure that authorities took some steps towards justice and accountability. On 3 September, the government\n[189]\nannounced that six Republican Guards, including the Republican Guard commander for North Kivu and his deputy, had been arrested and would be brought to trial.\n[190]\nIn addition, the government announced that the governor and operations commander for North Kivu, Lieutenant-General Constant Ndima, had been “immediately recalled to Kinshasa for consultations” and that two police officials in Goma were suspended from their duties and also recalled to Kinshasa.\n[191]\n\nIn a press release published after the killings in Goma, MONUSCO said it “remains concerned by the threats of violence made prior to the demonstration.”\nThe trial of the two officers and four Republican Guard soldiers was held before the North Kivu Military Court in Goma from 4 September to 3 October 2023. They were charged with crimes against humanity by murder and violating orders.\n[192]\nOn 2 October, the court handed down its judgment. The commander of the Republican Guard in North Kivu, Colonel Mike Kalamba Mikombe, was found guilty and sentenced to death for “murder”, “attempted murder”, and “destruction of armaments.” He was also dismissed from the armed forces.\n[193]\nThe Special Forces Regiment Commander and one soldier were acquitted, while three other soldiers were sentenced to 10 years in prison for “murder” and “attempted murder.”\n[194]\nIn addition, the court recognized 109 people as victims and ordered Mike Mikombe and the three soldiers to pay them the equivalent of 100,000 US dollars for reparations\n[195]\n(917 US dollars per person on average). However, the court did not recognize the FNJMN/Wazalendo as a legal entity capable of bringing claims before a court of law, and as a victim.\n[196]\nThe court also rejected Efraimu Bisimwa’s claim for reparations on the grounds of “immorality.”\n[197]\nThe investigations carried out by the authorities on both the murder of the police officer and the unlawful killings of the Wazalendo followers were marred by serious shortcomings:\n[198]\nNo investigators were sent to the shooting scenes to collect evidence.\n[199]\nBy the military prosecutor’s own admission, there were no ballistic investigations or autopsies (while the DRC has limited capabilities in this area, a UN official told Amnesty International that they expressed readiness to provide technical assistance, but the authorities did not seem interested in the offer).\n[200]\nThe military prosecutor said: “We did not request UN assistance for autopsies, but we did for ballistic investigation. I think they sent an expert from Kinshasa – I caught a glimpse of him here – but unfortunately, I had a lot of setbacks, and we ended up not meeting.”\n[201]\nAmnesty International believes the investigation failed to identify all those who should have been investigated for their possible responsibility.\nThe investigation also failed to identify the names, ages, genders, addresses and family members of all 56 officially recognized dead. Moreover, some victims are still missing.
